font-weight: 500; font-size: 28px; line-height: 24px; margin: 44px 0px 30px;"><span style="font-size: 18pt;">Word of the Day</span></div><div style="color: #44475b; border: 1px solid grey; border-radius: 8px; text-align: center; width: 400px; max-width: 100%; margin: 0px auto 28px;"><div style="font-weight: 600; font-size: 20px; line-height: 24px; margin-top: 28px;"><span style="font-size: 12pt;"><span style="font-family: 'Open Sans', sans-serif;"><span style="font-size: 14pt; color: #000000;">Cash Conversion Cycle</span></span><br><br></span></div><div style="width: 100%; height: 0px; border-top: 1px solid grey;"><br></div><div style="font-weight: 400; padding: 32px; text-align: center; font-size: 16px; line-height: 22px;"><p>It shows how long it takes a company to turn its investments into cash</p></div></div><div style="color: #44475b; font-weight: 400; font-size: 11pt; line-height: 22px; margin-bottom: 16px;"><p>It measures the time from buying materials, making products, selling them, to collecting money from customers.</p><p>The time taken is measured in days.</p><p>A shorter cycle means the company gets cash back faster, which shows it is running efficiently.</p><p>For companies that keep products or materials in stock (inventory), this is one of the metrics of their financial health.</p></div><div style="color: #44475b; font-weight: 500; font-size: 28px; line-height: 24px; margin: 44px 0px 30px;"><span style="font-size: 18pt;">6 Day Course</span></div><div style="color: #44475b; font-weight: 500; font-size: 20px; margin-bottom: 20px; line-height: 24px;"><span style="font-size: 12pt;">Theme: company designations</span></div><div style="color: #44475b; width: 100%; height: 0px; border-top: 1px solid grey; margin-bottom: 10px;"><br></div><!--Thursday Start--><div style="font-weight: 500; font-size: 14px; line-height: 24px;"><span style="font-size: 11pt;"><span style="margin: 0px 10px;">Mon</span><span style="margin: 0px 10px;">Tue</span><span style="margin: 0px 10px;">Wed</span><span style="margin: 0px 10px; background: #44475b; color: white; border: 1px solid #44475b; border-radius: 30px; padding: 4px 8px;">Thu</span><span style="margin: 0px 10px; color: #a1a3ad;">Fri</span><span style="margin: 0px 10px; color: #a1a3ad;">Sun</span></span></div><!--Thursday End--><div style="color: #44475b; width: 100%; height: 0px; border-top: 1px solid grey; margin-top: 10px;"><br></div><div style="color: #44475b; font-weight: 400; font-size: 11pt; line-height: 22px; margin: 20px 0px 16px;"><p>Some mandatory roles are auditing roles.</p><p>Financial auditor: this role can be served by an Accountant or by a firm of Chartered Accountants.</p><p>This auditor is responsible for auditing financial records like balance sheets, profit and loss statements, cash flows, revenue, etc.</p><p>Internal auditor: this role can also be done by an individual or a firm.</p><p>The role audits operational processes like purchases, sales, inventory, etc.</p><p>Internal controls like segregation of duties, approvals, etc are also looked at by internal auditors.</p><p>Secretarial auditor: this role mainly checks if the company is fully complying with laws, regulations, and other such laws that might apply to companies belonging to certain industries/sectors.</p><p>Some companies might have a few other auditors that are mandatory based on the sector that they operate in.</p><p>Example: companies operating in the manufacturing, mining, metals, etc sector need to have a cost auditor who keeps a detailed eye on the expenses being incurred.</p><p>The quality and integrity of the people in the auditor roles is extremely important as the audits done by them are a direct source of truth about a company’s health for investors.</p></div><div style="color: #44475b; font-weight: 500; font-size: 28px; line-height: 24px; margin: 44px 0px 28px;"><span style="font-size: 18pt;">Featured Question</span></div><table style="color: #44475b;"><tbody><tr><td style="width: 20%; max-width: 180px;"></td><td style="border: 1px solid #7c7e8c; border-radius: 12px 12px 0px; padding: 20px 16px; font-weight: 500; font-size: 16px; line-height: 20px;"><span style="font-size: 11pt;">Q. “Can we transfer equity mutual funds to my son without selling them. How capital gain tax is calculated”<br></span></td></tr></tbody></table><div style="color: #44475b; border: 1px solid #e9e9eb; border-radius: 12px 12px 12px 0px; padding: 20px 16px; margin: 20px 0px; width: 70%; max-width: 420px;"><div style="font-weight: 400; font-size: 11pt; line-height: 22px; margin-bottom: 16px; color: #44475b;"><span style="font-size: 11pt;">Yes, it is possible.</span><p>When a gift is made from a father to the son, no gift tax is applicable. So this tax is not considered here.</p><p>This is done without selling the units or withdrawing money from the mutual fund. It happens via transfer of units.</p><p>The procedure to transfer equity mutual funds is different depending on if the mutual funds are stored in demat form or SOA form.</p><p>This will depend on the mutual fund platform you’re using.</p><p>The taxation occurs only at the time of withdrawing the money from the mutual fund. So tax is due when the son withdraws money from the mutual fund.</p><p>In this case, while calculating tax, capital gains and date of investment are calculated using the original investment date (when the father invested originally).</p></div></div><div style="color: #44475b; line-height: 22px; margin-bottom: 16px;"><ul style="padding-left: 24px;"><li><p>Airtel: company’s promoter group firm, Indian Continent Investment, sold a 0.56% stake worth Rs 7,200 crore to long-term investors.</p></li><li><p>Union Bank of India: confirmed it has timely paid Rs 71.8 crore annual interest on its Rs 1,000 crore bond on 26 Nov. It was also fined Rs 8.43 lakh by the RBI for deficiencies in soiled-note remittances and ATM cash-out issues.</p></li><li><p>Asian Paints: a step-down subsidiary will invest AED 140 million (around Rs 340 crore) to set up a second paint plant in Abu Dhabi’s KEZAD.</p></li><li><p>Havells India: will invest Rs 5.63 crore to acquire a 26% stake in Kundan Solar (Pali) Pvt Ltd to secure solar power and cut energy costs.</p></li><li><p>Info Edge: will invest Rs 5 crore in Smartweb and Rs 70 crore in Startup Investments, both wholly-owned subsidiaries, to support their funding and investment activities.</p></li><li><p>Axis Bank: allotted Rs 5,000 crore worth non-convertible debentures at 7.27% interest rate and a 10-year tenure on a private placement basis.</p></li></ul></div><div style="color: #44475b; font-weight: 500; font-size: 28px; line-height: 24px; margin: 44px 0px 30px;"><span style="font-size: 18pt;">Word of the Day</span></div><div style="color: #44475b; border: 1px solid grey; border-radius: 8px; text-align: center; width: 400px; max-width: 100%; margin: 0px auto 28px;"><div style="font-weight: 600; font-size: 20px; line-height: 24px; margin-top: 28px;"><span style="font-size: 12pt;"><span style="font-family: 'Open Sans', sans-serif;"><span style="font-size: 14pt; color: #000000;">Disposable Income</span></span><br><br></span></div><div style="width: 100%; height: 0px; border-top: 1px solid grey;"><br></div><div style="font-weight: 400; padding: 32px; text-align: center; font-size: 16px; line-height: 22px;"><p>It is the money that is left after paying taxes</p></div></div><div style="color: #44475b; font-weight: 400; font-size: 11pt; line-height: 22px; margin-bottom: 16px;"><p>When a person earns income, a portion of it goes toward taxes or other mandatory payments.</p><p>Whatever remains after these payments is called disposable income.</p><p>This money can be used for daily expenses, savings, or personal lifestyle choices.</p><p>A higher disposable income allows the person to have more financial freedom.</p></div><div style="color: #44475b; font-weight: 500; font-size: 28px; line-height: 24px; margin: 44px 0px 30px;"><span style="font-size: 18pt;">6 Day Course</span></div><div style="color: #44475b; font-weight: 500; font-size: 20px; margin-bottom: 20px; line-height: 24px;"><span style="font-size: 12pt;">Theme: company designations</span></div><div style="color: #44475b; width: 100%; height: 0px; border-top: 1px solid grey; margin-bottom: 10px;"><br></div><!--Wednesday Start--><div style="font-weight: 500; font-size: 14px; line-height: 24px;"><span style="font-size: 11pt;"><span style="margin: 0px 10px;">Mon</span><span style="margin: 0px 10px;">Tue</span><span style="margin: 0px 10px; background: #44475b; color: white; border: 1px solid #44475b; border-radius: 30px; padding: 4px 8px;">Wed</span><span style="margin: 0px 10px; color: #a1a3ad;">Thu</span><span style="margin: 0px 10px; color: #a1a3ad;">Fri</span><span style="margin: 0px 10px; color: #a1a3ad;">Sun</span></span></div><!--Wednesday End--><div style="color: #44475b; width: 100%; height: 0px; border-top: 1px solid grey; margin-top: 10px;"><br></div><div style="color: #44475b; font-weight: 400; font-size: 11pt; line-height: 22px; margin: 20px 0px 16px;"><p>Now lets talk about the directors.</p><p>Every listed company must have a board. The board consists of directors.</p><p>One of the board's primary functions is to look after the shareholders' interests (not just promoters').</p><p>It appoints the senior management of a company including the CEO.</p><p>Large listed companies (top 2000 biggest) must have 6 directors or more.<br>One-third of the directors must be independent. (directors who are not executives in the company).</p><p>The board is responsible for deciding the general direction the company takes, appointing and keeping a check on senior executives, protecting shareholder rights, and legal compliance.</p></div><div style="color: #44475b; font-weight: 500; font-size: 28px; line-height: 24px; margin: 44px 0px 28px;"><span style="font-size: 18pt;">Featured Question</span></div><table style="color: #44475b;"><tbody><tr><td style="width: 20%; max-width: 180px;"></td><td style="border: 1px solid #7c7e8c; border-radius: 12px 12px 0px; padding: 20px 16px; font-weight: 500; font-size: 16px; line-height: 20px;"><span style="font-size: 11pt;">Q. “Few days back I read an article that many countries are having loan above 100% of their GDP. Many others have nearly 70-80% of their GDP. If almost all the countries have loans. Who provide the loans as the institutions like IMF and World Bank are funded by these big countries.”<br></span></td></tr></tbody></table><div style="color: #44475b; border: 1px solid #e9e9eb; border-radius: 12px 12px 12px 0px; padding: 20px 16px; margin: 20px 0px; width: 70%; max-width: 420px;"><div style="font-weight: 400; font-size: 11pt; line-height: 22px; margin-bottom: 16px; color: #44475b;"><span style="font-size: 11pt;">Countries' governments get money in two main ways.</span><p>Taxes and loans.</p><p>When the government needs money to spend on infrastructure, defence, education, etc, it tries to raise that money from taxes.</p><p>But when a large spending requirement comes up suddenly, the government cannot just raise taxes.</p><p>This is why besides tax, they also take loans.</p><p>These loans can be from other countries, banks, and even their own citizens!</p><p>How does that happen?</p><p>The government offers bonds for investment. These bonds pay principal + interest.</p><p>This is basically a loan the government takes.</p><p>Most large countries keep taking loans and paying them back. It's a continuous process.</p><p>When the loans/debt is too high, it gets risky. But some level of debt is considered healthy.</p><p>India's debt is considered to be in the healthy zone.</p><p>Japan and the USA are in the uncomfortable zone.</p></div></div><div style="color: #44475b; width: 5%; height: 0px; border-top: 3px solid black; margin: 24px 0px 16px;"><br></div><div style="color: #7c7e8c; font-style: italic; margin-bottom: 8px; font-weight: 400; font-size: 14px; line-height: 20px;"><span style="font-size: 11pt;">In this section, daily, we'll try to answer one burning question related to the field of personal finance education.</span></div><div style="color: #7c7e8c; font-style: italic; margin-bottom: 8px; font-weight: 400; font-size: 14px; line-height: 20px;"><span style="font-size: 11pt;">Remember, NO investment advice &amp; ONLY personal finance education questions.</span></div><div style="color: #7c7e8c; font-weight: 500; font-size: 28px; line-height: 24px; margin: 44px 0px 30px;"><span style="font-size: 18pt;">Word of the Day</span></div><div style="color: #44475b; border: 1px solid grey; border-radius: 8px; text-align: center; width: 400px; max-width: 100%; margin: 0px auto 28px;"><div style="font-weight: 600; font-size: 20px; line-height: 24px; margin-top: 28px;"><span style="font-size: 12pt;"><span style="font-family: 'Open Sans', sans-serif;"><span style="font-size: 14pt; color: #000000;">Public Float</span></span><br><br></span></div><div style="width: 100%; height: 0px; border-top: 1px solid grey;"><br></div><div style="font-weight: 400; padding: 32px; text-align: center; font-size: 16px; line-height: 22px;"><p>It is the portion of a company’s shares that are freely available for trading in the share market</p></div></div><div style="color: #44475b; font-weight: 400; font-size: 11pt; line-height: 22px; margin-bottom: 16px;"><p>These shares exclude promoter holdings, government stakes, or any locked-in shares.</p><p>A higher public float usually means more liquidity, making it easier for retail investors to buy or sell the share, without significantly impacting the share price.</p><p>It is used to understand a company’s ownership structure.</p></div><div style="color: #44475b; font-weight: 500; font-size: 28px; line-height: 24px; margin: 44px 0px 30px;"><span style="font-size: 18pt;">6 Day Course</span></div><div style="color: #44475b; font-weight: 500; font-size: 20px; margin-bottom: 20px; line-height: 24px;"><span style="font-size: 12pt;">Theme: company designations</span></div><div style="color: #44475b; width: 100%; height: 0px; border-top: 1px solid grey; margin-bottom: 10px;"><br></div><!--Tuesday Start--><div style="font-weight: 500; font-size: 14px; line-height: 24px;"><span style="font-size: 11pt;"><span style="margin: 0px 10px;">Mon</span><span style="margin: 0px 10px; background: #44475b; color: white; border: 1px solid #44475b; border-radius: 30px; padding: 4px 8px;">Tue</span><span style="margin: 0px 10px; color: #a1a3ad;">Wed</span><span style="margin: 0px 10px; color: #a1a3ad;">Thu</span><span style="margin: 0px 10px; color: #a1a3ad;">Fri</span><span style="margin: 0px 10px; color: #a1a3ad;">Sun</span></span></div><!--Tuesday End--><div style="color: #44475b; width: 100%; height: 0px; border-top: 1px solid grey; margin-top: 10px;"><br></div><div style="color: #44475b; font-weight: 400; font-size: 11pt; line-height: 22px; margin: 20px 0px 16px;"><p>Every listed company must have a Managing Director (MD) or Chief Executive Officer (CEO).</p><p>In addition to that, they also need to have a Chief Financial Officer (CFO) and Compliance Officer.</p><p>The Compliance Officer’s role is usually done by the Company Secretary.</p><p>The CFO is responsible for financial matters like raising funding (via loans or selling shares), allocating capital to different functions, and managing financial risk.</p><p>The CFO has the legal power to veto the CEO’s/board's spending plans.</p><p>This role makes the CFO personally liable.</p><p>In case of financial irregularities and cases of fraud, the CFO can go to jail.</p></div><div style="color: #44475b; font-weight: 500; font-size: 28px; line-height: 24px; margin: 44px 0px 28px;"><span style="font-size: 18pt;">Featured Question</span></div><table style="color: #44475b;"><tbody><tr><td style="width: 20%; max-width: 180px;"></td><td style="border: 1px solid #7c7e8c; border-radius: 12px 12px 0px; padding: 20px 16px; font-weight: 500; font-size: 16px; line-height: 20px;"><span style="font-size: 11pt;">Q. “What is the FIFO, first in first out method in the share market and how can I understand it by an example?”<br></span></td></tr></tbody></table><div style="color: #44475b; border: 1px solid #e9e9eb; border-radius: 12px 12px 12px 0px; padding: 20px 16px; margin: 20px 0px; width: 70%; max-width: 420px;"><div style="font-weight: 400; font-size: 11pt; line-height: 22px; margin-bottom: 16px; color: #44475b;"><span style="font-size: 11pt;">It is a method using which stocks are sold.</span><p>Let’s say you bought a company’s stocks multiple times over different periods.</p><p>When you try to sell this stock, the oldest stocks (the ones you bought first) will be sold.</p><p>First In, First Out.</p><p>Since tax on longer term investments is lower, selling shares that have been held the longest can reduce tax.</p><p>Example:</p><p>You bought 100 shares of XYZ in Jan 2020 at Rs 45<br>You bought 400 shares of XYZ in March 2020 at Rs 56<br>You bought 200 shares of XYZ in April 2022 at Rs 72</p><p>In Aug 2025, you sell 200 shares of XYZ.</p><p>So, first, the 100 shares you bought in January 2020 will be sold.</p><p>Next, 100 out of 400 shares you bought in March 2020 will be sold.</p></div></div><div style="color: #44475b; width: 5%; height: 0px; border-top: 3px solid black; margin: 24px 0px 16px;"><br></div><div style="color: #7c7e8c; font-style: italic; margin-bottom: 8px; font-weight: 400; font-size: 14px; line-height: 20px;"><span style="font-size: 11pt;">In this section, daily, we'll try to answer one burning question related to the field of personal finance education.</span></div><div style="color: #7c7e8c; font-style: italic; margin-bottom: 8px; font-weight: 400; font-size: 14px; line-height: 20px;"><span style="font-size: 11pt;">Remember, NO investment advice &amp; ONLY personal finance education questions.</span></div><div style="color: #7c7e8c; font-weight: 500; font-size: 28px; line-height: 24px; margin: 44px 0px 30px;"><span style="font-size: 18pt;">Word of the Day</span></div><div style="color: #44475b; border: 1px solid grey; border-radius: 8px; text-align: center; width: 400px; max-width: 100%; margin: 0px auto 28px;"><div style="font-weight: 600; font-size: 20px; line-height: 24px; margin-top: 28px;"><span style="font-size: 12pt;"><span style="font-family: 'Open Sans', sans-serif;"><span style="font-size: 14pt; color: #000000;">Debt Financing</span></span><br><br></span></div><div style="width: 100%; height: 0px; border-top: 1px solid grey;"><br></div><div style="font-weight: 400; padding: 32px; text-align: center; font-size: 16px; line-height: 22px;"><p>It is when a company raises money by borrowing it</p></div></div><div style="color: #44475b; font-weight: 400; font-size: 11pt; line-height: 22px; margin-bottom: 16px;"><p>This is usually done through loans or by issuing bonds.</p><p>The borrowed amount is paid back over time, with interest. This way the company can raise the funds it needs without giving away ownership.</p><p>The company must ensure it can repay the debt on time to avoid financial problems.</p></div><div style="color: #44475b; font-weight: 500; font-size: 28px; line-height: 24px; margin: 44px 0px 30px;"><span style="font-size: 18pt;">6 Day Course</span></div><div style="color: #44475b; font-weight: 500; font-size: 20px; margin-bottom: 20px; line-height: 24px;"><span style="font-size: 12pt;">Theme: different kinds of buyers/sellers in share market</span></div><div style="color: #44475b; width: 100%; height: 0px; border-top: 1px solid grey; margin-bottom: 10px;"><br></div><!--Friday Start--><div style="font-weight: 500; font-size: 14px; line-height: 24px;"><span style="font-size: 11pt;"><span style="margin: 0px 10px;">Mon</span><span style="margin: 0px 10px;">Tue</span><span style="margin: 0px 10px;">Wed</span><span style="margin: 0px 10px;">Thu</span><span style="margin: 0px 10px; background: #44475b; color: white; border: 1px solid #44475b; border-radius: 30px; padding: 4px 8px;">Fri</span><span style="margin: 0px 10px; color: #a1a3ad;">Sun</span></span></div><!--Friday End--><div style="color: #44475b; width: 100%; height: 0px; border-top: 1px solid grey; margin-top: 10px;"><br></div><div style="color: #44475b; font-weight: 400; font-size: 11pt; line-height: 22px; margin: 20px 0px 16px;"><p>One big category of buyers and sellers is proprietary trading desks.</p><p>These traders employ a host of different trading strategies like arbitrage, high frequency trading, shorting, speculative bets, etc.</p><p>The sheer volume of their trading activity ensures ample liquidity in the markets resulting in easy buying/selling for other investors.</p><p>Next, let’s talk about passive investors. Passive investors are FPIs or DIIs that invest based on a rule.</p><p>Nifty 50 index fund being an example.</p><p>In case of big index funds, large amounts of money get invested when a certain rule is met.</p><p>Eg: when a new company is added to an index.</p><p>With that, we have covered most of the broad categories of investors.</p><p>There can be many classifications and sub-classifications inside these categories.</p><p>Each of their behaviour can vary a bit too.</p></div><div style="color: #44475b; font-weight: 500; font-size: 28px; line-height: 24px; margin: 44px 0px 28px;"><span style="font-size: 18pt;">Featured Question</span></div><table style="color: #44475b;"><tbody><tr><td style="width: 20%; max-width: 180px;"></td><td style="border: 1px solid #7c7e8c; border-radius: 12px 12px 0px; padding: 20px 16px; font-weight: 500; font-size: 16px; line-height: 20px;"><span style="font-size: 11pt;">Q. “When market is unpredictable, is it not better to invest through mutual fund rather than through equity”<br></span></td></tr></tbody></table><div style="color: #44475b; border: 1px solid #e9e9eb; border-radius: 12px 12px 12px 0px; padding: 20px 16px; margin: 20px 0px; width: 70%; max-width: 420px;"><div style="font-weight: 400; font-size: 11pt; line-height: 22px; margin-bottom: 16px; color: #44475b;"><span style="font-size: 11pt;">It all comes down to individual skill and knowledge.</span><p>Even fund managers running mutual funds are human beings like everyone else.</p><p>The stock markets are unpredictable in the short run.</p><p>Because of this, it is easy to lose money. But it also presents many opportunities to buy stocks at good prices.</p><p>So, if an investor is good at picking stocks, this unpredictable nature of the markets can be an opportunity.</p><p>Else, it can become a risk.</p><p>In cases where an investor does not have the required skill and knowledge, it is better to invest in a mutual fund where a fund manager decides what to do.</p></div></div><div style="color: #44475b; width: 5%; height: 0px; border-top: 3px solid black; margin: 24px 0px 16px;"><br></div><div style="color: #7c7e8c; font-style: italic; margin-bottom: 8px; font-weight: 400; font-size: 14px; line-height: 20px;"><span style="font-size: 11pt;">In this section, daily, we'll try to answer one burning question related to the field of personal finance education.</span></div><div style="color: #7c7e8c; color: #000000;">Trading Account</span></span><br><br></span></div><div style="width: 100%; height: 0px; border-top: 1px solid grey;"><br></div><div style="font-weight: 400; padding: 32px; text-align: center; font-size: 16px; line-height: 22px;"><p>It is an account that is used to buy and sell shares and other securities in the market</p></div></div><div style="color: #44475b; font-weight: 400; font-size: 11pt; line-height: 22px; margin-bottom: 16px;"><p>Just like a demat account stores your investments, a trading account is used to place orders. It executes the buy and sell transactions.</p><p>When you buy, money is debited from the trading account and when you sell, money is credited back to the trading account.&nbsp;</p><p>The trading account acts as a bridge between the bank account and the demat account.</p></div><div style="color: #44475b; font-weight: 500; font-size: 28px; line-height: 24px; margin: 44px 0px 30px;"><span style="font-size: 18pt;">6 Day Course</span></div><div style="color: #44475b; font-weight: 500; font-size: 20px; margin-bottom: 20px; line-height: 24px;"><span style="font-size: 12pt;">Theme: different kinds of buyers/sellers in share market</span></div><div style="color: #44475b; width: 100%; height: 0px; border-top: 1px solid grey; margin-bottom: 10px;"><br></div><!--Tuesday Start--><div style="font-weight: 500; font-size: 14px; line-height: 24px;"><span style="font-size: 11pt;"><span style="margin: 0px 10px;">Mon</span><span style="margin: 0px 10px; background: #44475b; color: white; border: 1px solid #44475b; border-radius: 30px; padding: 4px 8px;">Tue</span><span style="margin: 0px 10px; color: #a1a3ad;">Wed</span><span style="margin: 0px 10px; color: #a1a3ad;">Thu</span><span style="margin: 0px 10px; color: #a1a3ad;">Fri</span><span style="margin: 0px 10px; color: #a1a3ad;">Sun</span></span></div><!--Tuesday End--><div style="color: #44475b; width: 100%; height: 0px; border-top: 1px solid grey; margin-top: 10px;"><br></div><div style="color: #44475b; font-weight: 400; font-size: 11pt; line-height: 22px; margin: 20px 0px 16px;"><p>FPIs — Foreign Portfolio Investors.</p><p>These are investors based abroad but investing in the Indian stock markets.</p><p>This category includes all kinds of overseas-based institutional investors. It can include insurance companies, mutual funds, index funds, hedge funds, etc.</p><p>These investors are usually very stable in their investment style — they do not react as rapidly to good/bad news.</p><p>They usually invest larger amounts and are capable of causing large swings in share prices with their buying/selling.</p><p>International factors greatly affect their decisions. This includes geopolitical factors, exchange rates, and interest rates in the US and other big western economies.</p><p>For this reason, Indian investors like to keep an eye on the activities of FPIs. It may sometimes give some idea of what is happening in the markets.</p></div><div style="color: #44475b; font-weight: 500; font-size: 28px; line-height: 24px; margin: 44px 0px 28px;"><span style="font-size: 18pt;">Featured Question</span></div><table style="color: #44475b;"><tbody><tr><td style="width: 20%; max-width: 180px;"></td><td style="border: 1px solid #7c7e8c; border-radius: 12px 12px 0px; padding: 20px 16px; font-weight: 500; font-size: 16px; line-height: 20px;"><span style="font-size: 11pt;">Q. “Please explain Value investing vs growth investing. Hiw does one identify which stock is value /growth?”<br></span></td></tr></tbody></table><div style="color: #44475b; border: 1px solid #e9e9eb; border-radius: 12px 12px 12px 0px; padding: 20px 16px; margin: 20px 0px; width: 70%; max-width: 420px;"><div style="font-weight: 400; font-size: 11pt; line-height: 22px; margin-bottom: 16px; color: #44475b;"><p>There are many stock picking strategies. These two are the most talked about strategies.</p><p>Value investing:</p><p>The idea behind value investing is to pick up stocks for a price that is fair or lower.</p><p>Paying a high price for a stock is a no no.</p><p>Of course, the definition of ‘fair’ is different for different investors. It is subjective. There’s no clear formula for it.</p><p>Value investors tend to pay a lot of attention to the fundamentals like revenue, earnings, debt, cash flow, etc.</p><p>Growth investing:</p><p>The idea behind this is that it is okay to pay an overvalued price for a stock as long as it grows very fast.</p><p>If a stock price rises high enough, a slightly overvalued price is still going to lead to profits.</p><p>Growth investors tend to pay a lot more attention to revenue growth, future potential, ability to scale, total addressable market, ability of the management, etc.</p><p>They tend to be more comfortable with higher valuations/overvalued stocks.</p><p>Growth investing is considered riskier than value investing.</p><p>Value stocks vs growth stocks</p><p>- Growth stocks tend to have higher valuations (higher PE ratio, PB ratio, etc)</p><p>- Growth stocks' earnings growth is higher</p><p>- Volatility and risk is also higher in case of growth stocks</p><p>- Growth stocks tend to require a longer investment horizon from investors</p><p>- Value stocks tend to give higher dividends</p><p>In short: value investors want to pay a fair price for shares. Growth investors are betting on future potential.</p><p>Something investors need to understand is that there is no clear definition for what a growth stock is and what a value stock is.</p><p>It is a bit loosely discussed.</p><p>Many investors do not like these two words at all because their definitions can be vague.</p></div></div><div style="color: #44475b; width: 5%; height: 0px; border-top: 3px solid black; margin: 24px 0px 16px;"><br></div><div style="color: #7c7e8c; font-style: italic; margin-bottom: 8px; font-weight: 400; font-size: 14px; line-height: 20px;"><span style="font-size: 11pt;">In this section, daily, we'll try to answer one burning question related to the field of personal finance education.</span></div><div style="color: #7c7e8c; font-style: italic; margin-bottom: 8px; font-weight: 400; font-size: 14px; line-height: 20px;"><span style="font-size: 11pt;">Remember, NO investment advice &amp; ONLY personal finance education questions.</span></div><div style="color: #7c7e8c; font-weight: 500; font-size: 28px; line-height: 24px; margin: 44px 0px 30px;"><span style="font-size: 18pt;">Word of the Day</span></div><div style="color: #44475b; border: 1px solid grey; border-radius: 8px; text-align: center; width: 400px; max-width: 100%; margin: 0px auto 28px;"><div style="font-weight: 600; font-size: 20px; line-height: 24px; margin-top: 28px;"><span style="font-size: 12pt;"><span style="font-family: 'Open Sans', sans-serif;"><span style="font-size: 14pt; color: #000000;">Market Correction</span></span><br><br></span></div><div style="width: 100%; height: 0px; border-top: 1px solid grey;"><br></div><div style="font-weight: 400; padding: 32px; text-align: center; font-size: 16px; line-height: 22px;"><p>It is a decline in stock prices, from a recent high</p></div></div><div style="color: #44475b; font-weight: 400; font-size: 11pt; line-height: 22px; margin-bottom: 16px;"><p>They act as a cooling-off period, where prices adjust to lower levels.</p><p>Corrections are a normal part of market cycles.</p><p>Example: after Nifty 50 reached an all time high in Sept 2024, markets corrected around 18% till Apr 2025.&nbsp;</p></div><div style="color: #44475b; font-weight: 500; font-size: 28px; line-height: 24px; margin: 44px 0px 30px;"><span style="font-size: 18pt;">6 Day Course</span></div><div style="color: #44475b; font-weight: 500; font-size: 20px; margin-bottom: 20px; line-height: 24px;"><span style="font-size: 12pt;">Theme: bonds</span></div><div style="color: #44475b; width: 100%; height: 0px; border-top: 1px solid grey; margin-bottom: 10px;"><br></div><!--Thursday Start--><div style="font-weight: 500; font-size: 14px; line-height: 24px;"><span style="font-size: 11pt;"><span style="margin: 0px 10px;">Mon</span><span style="margin: 0px 10px;">Tue</span><span style="margin: 0px 10px;">Wed</span><span style="margin: 0px 10px; background: #44475b; color: white; border: 1px solid #44475b; border-radius: 30px; padding: 4px 8px;">Thu</span><span style="margin: 0px 10px; color: #a1a3ad;">Fri</span><span style="margin: 0px 10px; color: #a1a3ad;">Sun</span></span></div><!--Thursday End--><div style="color: #44475b; width: 100%; height: 0px; border-top: 1px solid grey; margin-top: 10px;"><br></div><div style="color: #44475b; font-weight: 400; font-size: 11pt; line-height: 22px; margin: 20px 0px 16px;"><p>We know that bonds are traded on the bond markets — similar to how stocks are traded on the stock markets.</p><p>The price of each bond depends on multiple factors:</p><p>1. Current interest rates being offered by newly issued bonds</p><p>2. Credit ratings or the ability of the bond issuer to pay on maturity</p><p>3. Current inflation rate</p><p>4. Liquidity or how easily a bond can be bought/sold</p><p>5. Time left to maturity</p><p>Many of these variables are unknown – nobody knows for sure what the future inflation will be, or how the interest rates will change, etc.</p><p>This makes it similar to stocks. Investors have to research, and develop informed opinions.</p><p>Since there are thousands of investors, each have their own opinion. They buy and sell based on those.</p></div><div style="color: #44475b; font-weight: 500; font-size: 28px; line-height: 24px; margin: 44px 0px 28px;"><span style="font-size: 18pt;">Featured Question</span></div><table style="color: #44475b;"><tbody><tr><td style="width: 20%; max-width: 180px;"></td><td style="border: 1px solid #7c7e8c; border-radius: 12px 12px 0px; padding: 20px 16px; font-weight: 500; font-size: 16px; line-height: 20px;"><span style="font-size: 11pt;">Q. “There are some companies listed in Nifty 50 whose for example: P/B is 10, P/E 1800, ROE 0.81%, EPS 0.24. Which compared to its sector valuations are highly overvalued. Explain how the valuation of these companies is calculated/justified, and if so why are these companies included in Nifty 50 when better alternatives are available?”<br></span></td></tr></tbody></table><div style="color: #44475b; border: 1px solid #e9e9eb; border-radius: 12px 12px 12px 0px; padding: 20px 16px; margin: 20px 0px; width: 70%; max-width: 420px;"><div style="font-weight: 400; font-size: 11pt; line-height: 22px; margin-bottom: 16px; color: #44475b;"><span style="font-size: 11pt;">Overvaluation can occur due to many reasons.</span><p>One of the simplest reasons is investor sentiments.</p><p>Sometimes, investors become extremely bullish about a company’s future and keep investing money into its stock.</p><p>This pushes the stock price up continuously leading to the higher price and overvaluation.</p><p>At other times, stocks become overvalued when their earnings, revenues fall sharply in a certain quarter — but investors think that the dip is temporary.</p><p>Let’s say a company’s profits are down, or it makes a loss.</p><p>But if investors feel the dip is temporary, they might not sell. So the price will remain roughly where it already was.</p><p>This is another way a company can become extremely overvalued.</p><p>This does not necessarily mean the stock’s earnings will bounce back. It means the investors expect it to bounce back.</p><p>Some companies become extremely overvalued simply because investors are willing to pay a high price in present times — because they expect the future price to be even higher.</p><p>Nifty 50 is a rule based index. The rule is that it is made up of 50 of the biggest companies in India.</p><p>Biggest companies = highest market cap.</p><p>So it gets made using this rule. It does not look at overvaluation to decide which stocks to include in it.</p><p>The market cap depends directly on the share price. So if a company share price rises enough (and therefore, its market cap), it will become a part of Nifty 50.</p><p>It does not matter if it is a loss making company or an overvalued company.</p></div></div><div style="color: #44475b; width: 5%; height: 0px; border-top: 3px solid black; margin: 24px 0px 16px;"><br></div><div style="color: #7c7e8c; font-style: italic; margin-bottom: 8px; font-weight: 400; font-size: 14px; line-height: 20px;"><span style="font-size: 11pt;">In this section, daily, we'll try to answer one burning question related to the field of personal finance education.</span></div><div style="color: #7c7e8c; font-style: italic; margin-bottom: 8px; font-weight: 400; font-size: 14px; line-height: 20px;"><span style="font-size: 11pt;">Remember, NO investment advice &amp; ONLY personal finance education questions.</span></div><div style="color: #7c7e8c;
